--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of Round 1:**

The mission rewards:
- **Before switch time**: being STATIONARY (stop = good)
- **After switch time**: being MOVING (move = good, specifically toward the green-lit wall)

The champion (score 9697.62) is a pure Stop FSM - this makes sense because before the switch, all robots are stopped (good), and after the switch, they stay stopped (bad). The switch happens between steps 400-600, and the run lasts 1200 steps. So roughly 400-600 steps of being stopped (good) and 600-800 steps of being stopped (bad).

A pure Stop machine gets: ~0 penalty before switch + ~20 robots × ~700 steps after switch = ~14000 penalty from movement phase... wait, let me reconsider. Score 9697 for pure Stop means: before switch (~500 steps avg) = 0 penalty (stopped = good), after switch (~700 steps avg) = 20 × 700 = 14000... but score is 9697. 

Actually: before switch each stopped robot adds 1/step, so ~20 × 500 = 10000 from before. After switch each moving robot adds 1/step, stopped adds 0. So pure Stop: ~20 × 500 = 10000 from before (robots stationary), 0 from after. Score ≈ 10000. That matches 9697!

So the ideal strategy: **Stop before switch, Move after switch**. The signal is the GREEN wall lighting up (perceivable via C=7 with color green=2 or similar). 

After the switch, robots should GoToColor green (color 2 = green? Let me check: colors {1,2,3,4,5,6} - green is likely 2) or just Explore.

The key insight: detect the green wall appearing (C=7, color=2 for green), then switch from Stop to Exploration/GoToColor.

**Strategy:**
- State 0: Stop (before switch)
- Transition: when green color perceived (C=7, l=2) → go to State 1
- State 1: GoToColor green (B=8, clr=2) or Exploration (B=0)

Let me design candidates around this insight.

**FSM designs:**

1. Champion (elitism): pure Stop
2. 2-state: Stop → GoToColor(green) on perceiving green
3. 2-state: Stop → Exploration on perceiving green  
4. 2-state: Stop → GoToColor(green) with probability trigger too
5. 3-state: Stop → GoToColor(green) → Exploration
6. Various explorations

Green color in ARGoS is typically color index 2. Let me use that.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 2:**

The champion remains pure Stop (9697). The best 2-state machines use:
- State 0: Stop, with transition triggered by **perceiving green color (C=7, l=2)**
- State 1: **Exploration (B=0)** with low rwm (20-50) performs BETTER than GoToColor (B=8, clr=2)

This is counterintuitive but makes sense: GoToColor moves robots toward the green wall, but after the switch robots need to be MOVING (any movement counts). Exploration with low rwm moves forward with gentle turns. GoToColor may cause robots that can't see the green wall to behave oddly.

The best 2-state machines:
- Rank 2: Stop → Exploration(rwm=50) on green perceived, p=0.8 (score 10886)
- Rank 3: Stop → Exploration(rwm=20) on green perceived, p=0.95 (score 11066)
- Rank 4: Stop → GoAwayColor(green) on green perceived, p=0.85 (score 11168) - interesting!

**Key insight:** The transition probability matters. p=0.8 with rwm=50 beats p=0.95 with rwm=20. Higher rwm (more straight-line movement) seems better. Also, GoAwayColor from green wall makes robots move away = still moving = good score after switch!

**Problems with GoToColor:** Once robots reach the green wall they stop pressing against it (less movement). GoAwayColor keeps them moving away. Exploration keeps them moving randomly.

**Strategy for Round 3:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Exploit best performers: vary rwm (30-80), vary transition probability (0.7-0.9)
3. Try GoAwayColor with different probabilities
4. Try higher rwm values (60-100)
5. Try 3-state: Stop → Explore → if stuck, re-explore
6. Consider that the transition probability shouldn't be too high (false triggers before switch) or too low (slow response after switch)

The perception of green (C=7, l=2) is the key signal. The low p (0.8) beats high p (0.95) perhaps because at p=0.95 some robots falsely transition before the switch (though floor is gray, not green... wait, C=7 is about camera perception of colored objects, not floor). So false triggers happen when robots see green-colored robots (LEDs). If robots in state 1 have green LEDs, that could cause false triggers.

Actually, looking at the champion's LED: --cle0 5 (magenta for Stop state). The moving state has --cle1 6 (cyan). So no green LEDs - false triggers come only from the actual green wall. This means higher probability is safer. But p=0.8 beats p=0.95... perhaps because at higher p, the transition happens more reliably but some stochasticity helps when only a few robots can see the wall initially.

Let me try a range of rwm values and probabilities, focusing on the Exploration behavior.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 3:**

The pattern is now very clear:
- **Champion**: Pure Stop (9697) - best ever
- **Best 2-state**: Stop → Exploration(rwm=100) on green perceived, p=0.8 (10790)
- **Trend**: Higher rwm values (100 > 80 > 60 > 50 > 30) are better for the moving state
- **Transition probability**: p=0.8 seems sweet spot (p=0.7 gave 10860, p=0.9 gave 10908)
- **GoToColor/GoAwayColor**: worse than pure Exploration
- **Extra transitions from Stop state**: hurt performance (rank 14 has extra random transition)

**Analysis:**
- Pure Stop: ~9697 (best ever, variance: 9697-10167 across runs)
- Best 2-state: ~10790 (consistently worse than pure Stop)

Wait - the pure Stop consistently scores around 9700-10200, while the best 2-state machines score 10790+. This means the 2-state machines are NOT beating pure Stop. The 2-state machines trigger movement after seeing green, but this movement phase adds MORE cost than the stopped phase saves... 

Actually re-reading: pure Stop scores ~9697 because stopped robots score 1/step before switch AND 0/step after switch (they're stopped, so no penalty). Wait no - after switch, moving robots score 1/step. Stopped robots score 0 after switch. So pure Stop only accumulates score BEFORE the switch: ~20 robots × ~500 steps = ~10000. That matches!

The 2-state machines that switch to Exploration after seeing green: they start moving AFTER the switch (good - 0 penalty), but some robots may not see green immediately, adding delay. Also the very low back-transition probability (0.003) means they stay in exploration mode... but wait, if they're moving after the switch that's 0 penalty. So why are 2-state machines WORSE than pure Stop?

**The issue**: Before the switch, some robots falsely see green (or the wall is visible before the switch?). No wait - the green wall only lights up AT the switch time. Before that, no green is visible. So false transitions shouldn't happen. But the 2-state machines score ~10790+ vs pure Stop ~9697...

The problem must be that after the green wall appears, not all robots can see it immediately. During the gap between switch and when robots detect/respond to green, they're still stopped (good - 0 penalty after switch). But some robots may start moving before others... actually moving after switch = 0 penalty. So it shouldn't matter.

Unless: the robots that transition to Exploration move randomly and may go BACK to Stop state via the small probability (0.003). Or maybe the issue is that with Exploration, robots occasionally stop at walls and the step count matters.

Actually, I think the key issue is **variance**: pure Stop has deterministic behavior (always 0 after switch), while 2-state has variance. The scores suggest 2-state is consistently worse.

Wait - I'm misreading the scoring! Let me re-read: "Before switch: score += 1 for every STATIONARY robot. After switch: score += 1 for every MOVING robot."

So STOP before switch = BAD (adds to score). STOP after switch = GOOD (adds 0).
MOVE before switch = GOOD (adds 0). MOVE after switch = BAD (adds to score).

So pure Stop = all robots stationary before switch → MAXIMUM pre-switch penalty. And after switch, all stopped → 0 penalty. Total = 20 × switch_time ≈ 20 × 500 = 10000. That matches!

The ideal machine would MOVE before switch and STOP after switch. But robots can't know when the switch happens! The green wall appears AT the switch. So:
- Before switch: robots should be MOVING (to avoid penalty)
- After switch: green appears → robots should STOP

So the optimal design is:
- Default state: MOVING (Exploration)
- On seeing green → STOP

This is the OPPOSITE of what I've been doing! The 2-state machines (Stop→Explore on green) are wrong because they're stopped before the switch (bad) and moving after (bad - they should stop again!).

Let me recalculate pure Stop score: 20 robots × ~500 steps (before switch, all stationary) = ~10000 penalty before switch, + 0 after switch = ~10000. This matches 9697 (slightly less because switch is random 400-600, avg 500).

For pure Exploration: 0 before switch (moving), but ~20 × 700 after switch (still moving) = ~14000. This matches rank 6-7 from round 1 (~13731).

For the IDEAL machine (Move before, Stop after green): 0 before switch + 0 after switch = ~0! But in practice, some delay in seeing green and transitioning.

So I need: **Exploration → Stop on seeing green (color 2)**!

FSM: --nstates 2 --s0 0 --rwm0 X --cle0 Y --n0 1 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 P --l0x0 2 --s1 1 --cle1 Z --n1 1 --n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 small

This should score much closer to 0! Let me design these now.

The back-transition from Stop→Explore should have very low probability (don't want to start moving again after stopping).

**Design parameters to explore:**
- rwm: 50-100 (higher = more straight movement = less time wasted turning)
- transition probability p on green: 0.8-1.0 (high, want quick response)
- Back-transition: very small (0.001-0.003)

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 4:**

The champion is now: Exploration(rwm=100) → Stop on green(p=0.9) + random(p=0.001), with Stop → random back-transition(p=0.001). Score: 9443.59.

**Critical insight confirmed**: The correct design is Move-first → Stop on green. This is better than pure Stop (9697) because it avoids the pre-switch penalty from stationary robots.

**What the champion has that others don't:**
- `--n0 2` with TWO transitions from Exploration: one on green (p=0.9) AND one random (p=0.001)
- The extra random transition from Exploration state seems to help slightly

**Observations:**
- rwm=100 > rwm=80 > rwm=60 > rwm=50 for the moving state (higher = straighter = better coverage)
- p=0.9 on green detection seems optimal (1.0 gives 10215, 0.8 gives 10209, 0.9 gives 10210 for single-transition variants)
- Back-transition from Stop should be very small (0.001)
- The champion's extra random transition (p=0.001) from Exploration state is the key differentiator

**Why the extra random transition helps**: A tiny random chance to also transition to Stop even without seeing green may help robots that are stuck near walls or corners where they can't see the green light - they eventually stop anyway.

**Strategy for Round 5:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Exploit: vary the random back-transition probability from Exploration (try 0.0005, 0.002, 0.003)
3. Exploit: try p=0.9 vs 0.85 vs 0.95 on green detection with the two-transition setup
4. Explore: add a 3rd state for robots that lose sight of green after stopping
5. Explore: try Repulsion as moving behavior (keeps robots spread out, all moving)
6. Explore: try adding neighbour-count transitions to help coordination

The champion's key feature: `--n0 2 --n0x0 0 --c0x0 7 --p0x0 0.9 --l0x0 2 --n0x1 0 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.001`

Let me focus on exploiting this topology with parameter variations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key breakthrough from Round 5:**

The new champion uses **Repulsion** (B=5) instead of Exploration as the moving state! Score: 8236.68 vs best Exploration: 9301.

**Why Repulsion works better than Exploration:**
- Repulsion (B=5) actively pushes robots apart → ensures ALL robots are moving (not just wandering)
- When robots are spread out in a group, repulsion keeps every robot moving continuously
- Exploration (B=0) has random turns that may occasionally cause robots to pause or move inefficiently
- Repulsion ensures maximum movement coverage before the switch → minimum pre-switch penalty

**Champion topology:**
- State 0: Repulsion(rep=2.0), 2 transitions: green perceived (p=0.9) → Stop, random (p=0.001) → Stop
- State 1: Stop, 1 transition: random (p=0.001) → Repulsion

**What to exploit:**
1. Vary rep gain: 1.0, 1.5, 2.5, 3.0, 4.0, 5.0
2. Vary green transition probability: 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0
3. Vary random back-transition: 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
4. Try Attraction as moving state (keeps robots together, all moving)
5. Try 3-state: Repulsion → Stop (on green) → Repulsion (if green lost)
6. Try adding neighbour-count transitions to Repulsion state

**Key observations:**
- The random p=0.001 transition from Exploration helps (rank 2 had p=0.0005 back from Stop → worse)
- Single transition from Stop (p=0.001 random) is better than two transitions
- rep=2.0 is the champion value, explore around it

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 6:**

The champion: Repulsion(rep=2) → Stop on green(p=0.9) + random(p=0.002), Stop → random(p=0.001). Score: 7671.40.

**Critical parameter differences:**
- Champion has `p0x1 = 0.002` (random transition from Repulsion state) - BETTER than 0.001 or 0.0005
- Rank 2 has `p1x0 = 0.0005` (back from Stop) - also good (7924)
- rep=2 is optimal center, but rep=4 (8276) and rep=1.5/1.0 (8349) are competitive
- p=0.9 on green is better than p=1.0 or p=0.85/0.95
- Three transitions from Repulsion state hurts (rank 13: 9770)
- The `p0x1` random transition value matters a lot: 0.002 > 0.001 > 0.0005

**What to vary:**
1. `p0x1` (random transition from Repulsion): try 0.003, 0.004, 0.005, 0.0015
2. `p1x0` (back from Stop): try 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
3. `rep0`: try 2.0 (champion), also 4.0 with p0x1=0.002
4. Green detection probability: stay at 0.9
5. Combine champion's p0x1=0.002 with other rep values

**Key insight:** The random transition from Repulsion state (p0x1) acts as a "timeout" - even robots that can't see the green wall eventually stop. Higher p0x1 means faster timeout. But too high means premature stopping before switch. p=0.002 means on average 1/(0.002) = 500 steps to timeout - exactly matching the expected switch time! This is why it works so well.

So the optimal p0x1 should match 1/expected_switch_time = 1/500 = 0.002. Let me also try values around this sweet spot.

**Topology to keep:** 2 states, Repulsion → Stop (green p=0.9 + random p=?), Stop → Repulsion (random p=?)

Let me vary p0x1 around 0.002 and p1x0 around 0.001, and also try different rep values with p0x1=0.002.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 7:**

Champion: rep=2, p_green=0.9, p_random_from_rep=0.002, p_back_from_stop=0.0005. Score: 6935.29.

**Critical parameter: p1x0 (back-transition from Stop to Repulsion)**
- p1x0=0.0005 → 6935 (champion - BEST)
- p1x0=0.001 → 7609 (second best cluster)
- p1x0=0.0015 → 8153
- p1x0=0.002 → 8484 (worst)

So **smaller p1x0 is better** - robots should stay in Stop state longer after transitioning. p=0.0005 means average 2000 steps to leave Stop, which is basically permanent for a 1200-step run. This makes sense: once stopped (after green appears), stay stopped.

**p0x1 (random timeout from Repulsion):**
- 0.002 → champion (6935)
- 0.0025 → 7664
- 0.003 → 7700
- 0.0015 → 7856
- 0.004 → 7928

Sweet spot is around 0.002. Expected switch at step 500, so 1/0.002=500 steps average timeout.

**p0x0 (green detection):**
- 0.9 → best
- 0.92 → 7609, 0.88 → 7616 (very close to 0.9)

**rep0 values:** 2 is best, but 3-5 with p0x1=0.002 are competitive.

**Strategy for Round 8:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Exploit: vary p1x0 below 0.0005 (try 0.0001, 0.0002, 0.0003)
3. Exploit: vary p0x1 around 0.002 (try 0.0018, 0.0022)
4. Combine: different rep values with p1x0=0.0005
5. Try p1x0=0 (truly absorbing Stop state - but need at least one transition, so minimum probability)
6. Explore: try 3-state with the champion topology as core

The key insight: p1x0=0.0005 means robots almost never leave Stop once there. Could p1x0 even smaller help? Let's try very small values. Also try p0x1 slightly tuned.

## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 8:**

Champion: rep=2, p_green=0.9, p_random_from_rep=0.002, p_back_from_stop=0.0001. Score: 6576.24.

**Clear monotonic trend for p1x0 (back-transition from Stop):**
- 0.0001 → 6576 (champion)
- 0.0002 → 6829
- 0.0003 → 6997
- 0.0004 → 7042
- 0.0005 → 7220

**Smaller p1x0 is consistently better** - robots should almost never leave Stop once there. Could p1x0=0 work? No, a transition must exist. But we could try even smaller: 0.00005, 0.00001.

**p0x1 (random timeout from Repulsion): 0.002 is optimal**
- 0.0022 → 7279
- 0.0018 → 7345
- 0.002 → champion

**rep0: 2 is best**, 3-5 are close with p1x0=0.0005.

**p0x0 (green detection): 0.9 is optimal** (0.88 and 0.92 give similar results ~7221 at p1x0=0.0005).

**Strategy for Round 9:**
1. Keep champion (elitism)
2. Exploit: try p1x0 even smaller: 0.00005, 0.00001, 0.00002
3. Exploit: vary p0x1 around 0.002 with champion's p1x0=0.0001
4. Exploit: vary rep0 with champion's p1x0=0.0001
5. Explore: try p0x0 variants with p1x0=0.0001
6. Try 3-state topology for robustness

The big question: does p1x0 even smaller (near 0) help further? The trend is clear so yes. Let's push it.

Also note: the champion's p0x1=0.002 means avg 500 steps before random stop (matching switch time). p1x0=0.0001 means avg 10000 steps to leave Stop - essentially permanent in a 1200-step run. This is perfect.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Key findings from Round 9:**

Champion: rep=2, p_green=0.9, p_random_from_rep=0.002, p_back_from_stop=0 (literally 0!). Score: 6369.24.

**The simulator accepted p1x0=0** - this means Stop is truly absorbing (robots never leave Stop once there). This is optimal because after the switch, robots should stay stopped forever.

**Clear ranking of p1x0:**
- 0 → 6369 (champion - best)
- 0.0001 → 6604 (multiple machines)
- Smaller is consistently better → 0 is the limit

**p0x1 (random timeout from Repulsion): 0.002 is optimal**
- 0.0021 → 6560
- 0.0019 → 6615
- 0.002 → 6604 (with p1x0=0.0001), champion uses 0.002

**rep0 with p1x0=0.0001:**
- rep=4 → 6472 (better than rep=2!)
- rep=3 → 6551
- rep=2 → 6604

So now with p1x0=0 fixed, we should explore rep values more carefully: rep=4 was second best last round.

**Strategy for Round 10 (final round):**
1. Keep champion (elitism) - p1x0=0, rep=2, p0x1=0.002, p_green=0.9
2. Exploit: try rep=4 with p1x0=0
3. Exploit: try rep=3, 5 with p1x0=0
4. Exploit: vary p0x1 around 0.002 with p1x0=0 and rep=2
5. Exploit: vary p_green around 0.9 with p1x0=0
6. Exploit: try rep=4 with p0x1 variants
7. Some exploration with different rep values + p0x1 combinations

The champion's topology is now well-established. This is the final round so focus on fine-tuning the best parameters.

Key combinations to try:
- rep=4, p1x0=0, p0x1=0.002, p_green=0.9 (rank 2 last round had rep=4 with p1x0=0.0001 → 6472)
- rep=2, p1x0=0, p0x1=0.0019/0.0021 (slight variations)
- rep=3, p1x0=0
- rep=5, p1x0=0
- rep=4, p1x0=0, p0x1 variations

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
